| 1 | Mutual-cognition for proactive human–robot collaboration: A mixed reality-enabled visual reasoning-based method | IISE Transactions (2024, Vol. 56, No. 10, pp. 1099-1111) | 10.1080/24725854.2024.2313647 | The paper proposes a Mixed Reality (MR) and visual reasoning-based method to achieve mutual-cognitive intelligence for Proactive Human-Robot Collaboration (HRC). It introduces an MR-enabled HRC architecture that monitors Digital Twin states, reasons co-working strategies, and provides cognitive services. A visual reasoning approach is utilized to learn scene interpretations from visual perception, dynamically constructing scene graphs to generate task planning strategies. Furthermore, a safe, ergonomic, and proactive robot motion planning algorithm is deployed, allowing robots to execute tasks while human operators receive intuitive MR guidance. The system is demonstrated through an electric vehicle battery (EVB) disassembly task. | • Graph Convolutional Networks (GCN): Three-layer attentional GCN for contextual information extraction.• Link Prediction: Sigmoid functions and matrix multiplication for relatedness scoring between node pairs.• Forward Kinematics: 5-DOF kinematic model of the human arm using rotation matrices to calculate ergonomic interactive spaces.• RULA (Rapid Upper Limb Assessment): Ergonomic rules for joint rotation ranges.• Motion Planning: Rapidly-exploring Random Tree (RRT) for proactive trajectory generation and collision avoidance. | • Hardware: Microsoft HoloLens 2, Azure Kinect, UR5 Robot, MiR100 Mobile Base.• Software/Frameworks: ROS (Robot Operating System), OpenPose, ResNet 50, OctoMap, Vuforia Toolbox, Gazebo (for Digital Twin), Tesla V100 GPU (for model training). | A prototype HRC system for the 11-stage disassembly task of aging Electric Vehicle Batteries (EVBs). The operator wears MR glasses to receive video guidance and safety zones, while the mobile robot proactively hands over tools, tests power, and recycles battery cells based on inferred human intentions. | • Achieves mutual-cognitive and empathetic teamwork.• Enhances human context-awareness via MR trajectory previews and safety visualizations.• Ensures safe, ergonomic, and proactive robot manipulation.• Transitions HRC from a stiff master-slave mode to a flexible, collaborative paradigm. | • Precision of co-working strategy generation can be improved (sample imbalance issues).• Visual estimation errors in OpenPose and human movement uncertainty can cause ergonomic test failures.• Feasibility is limited to EVB disassembly; requires testing on other industrial cases (e.g., engine assembly). |
|  2 |  HAR2bot: a human-centered augmented reality robot programming method with the awareness of cognitive load |  Journal of Intelligent Manufacturing (2024, Vol. 35, pp. 1985-2003) |  10.1007/s10845-023-02096-2 |  The paper presents HAR2bot, a novel Augmented Reality (AR) interface for robot programming designed to manage the operator's cognitive load. Using NASA's system design process and Cognitive Load Theory (CLT), the authors assess existing online/offline programming methods to derive design guidelines. HAR2bot keeps humans in the loop, allowing operators to define waypoints, adjust end-effector orientations, and select robot configurations using an AR interface. The system optimizes intrinsic load (by making invisible data visible), reduces extraneous load (via simple UI and instant simulation), and increases germane load (via pre-training). A 16-participant user study verifies its efficiency against traditional online programming methods. |  • Inverse Kinematics: IKFast analytical solver to compute all possible 6-DOF joint configurations for a given end-effector pose.• Configuration Distance Metric: Sum of angular variations of corresponding joints to rank and suggest configurations.• Statistical Analysis: Kolmogorov-Smirnov normality test, paired t-tests to evaluate completion times, success rates, and NASA-TLX/CLC cognitive load scores. |  • Hardware: Oculus Rift VR headset, ZED mini stereo camera, AUBO i5 Cobot, hand-held controllers.• Software/Frameworks: ROS (Robot Operating System), Unity 3D engine, Open Motion Planning Library (OMPL), MoveIt Package. |  An AR-based human-in-the-loop Cobot programming system. Users wear a VR/AR headset to drag-and-drop virtual waypoints, validate collision-free paths in a mixed-reality environment, and execute the path on a physical AUBO i5 robot. |  • Significantly higher efficiency and lower completion times compared to manual leading and pendant teaching.• Effectively reduces overall, intrinsic, and extraneous cognitive loads.• High safety perception among users due to visual/auditory warnings.• Does not require CAD models or 3D environmental reconstruction. |  • Visualization accuracy is unsatisfactory, making precise programming and collision detection difficult.• Interaction relies on hand-held controllers; users preferred bare-hand interaction.• The pre-training implementation is primitive and could be enhanced with AI. |
|  3 |  Multi-agent embodied AI: advances and future directions |  SCIENCE CHINA Information Sciences (2026, Vol. 69, Iss. 5, 151202:1-31) |  10.1007/s11432-025-4820-4 |  This is a comprehensive review paper systematically covering the transition from single-agent to multi-agent embodied AI. It introduces foundational concepts (MAS, RL, optimal control) and categorizes methodologies into three technical routes: control/planning, learning-based methods, and generative model-driven frameworks. For multi-agent settings, the paper details approaches for asynchronous collaboration, heterogeneous collaboration, and self-evolution in open environments. It extensively reviews the use of generative models (LLMs/VLMs) for multi-agent task allocation, distributed decision-making, and human-AI coordination. The paper concludes by providing a landscape of benchmarks and identifying 8 critical future research directions. |  • Optimal Control: Calculus of variations, Pontryagin’s maximum principle, Bellman’s principle, Model Predictive Control (MPC) objective functions.• Markov Decision Processes: MDP and Dec-POMDP formulations for single and multi-agent RL.• Imitation Learning: Behavior Cloning (cross-entropy loss), Maximum Entropy IRL, GAIL (minimax objective).• Generative Models: Transformer Attention mechanisms (Q, K, V matrices), Diffusion models (forward/reverse Gaussian noise processes), State Space Models (Mamba differential equations). |  • Algorithms/Frameworks: Deep RL (DQN, PPO, MAPPO, QMIX, MADDPG), CTDE paradigm.• Generative AI: Large Language Models (LLMs), Vision-Language Models (VLMs), Transformers, Diffusion Models, Mamba.• Simulators/Benchmarks: AI2-THOR, RoboTHOR, MuJoCo, Isaac Gym, CARLA, Habitat, ROS/ROS2. |  As a review paper, it does not propose a single MVP. Instead, it catalogs conceptual frameworks for multi-agent task allocation (e.g., SMART-LLM), distributed decision-making, and human-AI coordination, alongside a detailed table of 20+ multi-agent embodied AI benchmarks. |  • Provides a unified taxonomy of embodied AI methods across single and multi-agent settings.• Identifies unique multi-agent challenges (non-stationarity, credit assignment, partial observability).• Serves as a foundational guide for future research in open-environment multi-agent systems. |  • Highlights that current methods struggle with sim-to-real transfer in dynamic, open environments.• Existing benchmarks lack multimodal signals and heterogeneous multi-agent complexity.• Theoretical stability and interpretability of integrating LLMs into multi-agent systems remain unresolved. |
|  4 |  Adaptive hybrid reasoning for agent-based digital twins of distributed multi-robot systems |  Simulation: Transactions of the Society for Modeling and Simulation International (2024) |  10.1177/00375497231226436 |  The paper introduces an approach for deploying agent-based Digital Twins (DTs) for Cyber-Physical Systems (CPS), specifically Multi-Robot Systems (MRS). It extends Zeigler's Modeling and Simulation Framework (MSF) with eight entities, including Physical Agents (PAs) and Digital Agents (DAs). To handle complex dynamic environments, a two-tier reasoning strategy is proposed: combining reactive agents (for real-time, stimulus-driven actions) and rational BDI (Belief-Desire-Intention) agents (for long-term, goal-oriented planning). A simulation and control platform was implemented to demonstrate how physical robots can be controlled via their DTs, enabling adaptive planning and decentralized decision-making. |  Pseudo-mathematical notation for agent behaviors (e.g., Agent reactive mapping inputs to actions).Set theory for representing Beliefs (B), Desires (D), and Intentions (I).BDI logic functions: UpdateBeliefs(), SelectDesire(), FormIntention(), and ExecutePlan(). |  JADE (Java Agent Development Framework)FIPA ACL (Agent Communication Language)MQTT (for high-volume real-time data)UWB (Ultra-Wide-Band for indoor localization)LEGO (for miniaturized physical prototyping) |  A simulation and control platform for an MRS where physical robots are represented as PAs, their DTs are created as DAs, and a Reasoning Agent assigns either reactive or BDI reasoning models on the fly to control the robots based on real-time environmental data. |  1\. Combines real-time responsiveness (reactive) with long-term planning (BDI).2\. Highly modular, scalable, and robust due to the decentralized MAS architecture.3\. Avoids single points of failure by separating physical and digital asset layers.4\. Enables on-the-fly switching of reasoning models based on context. |  1\. High complexity in designing and implementing BDI rational agents compared to simple reactive ones.2\. Potential for decision conflicts between reactive and rational models if not orchestrated properly.3\. Physical deployment is restricted by the need for UWB anchor infrastructure. |
|  5 |  Mixed-Reality Based Multi-Agent Robotics Framework for Artificial Swarm Intelligence Experiments |  IEEE Access (2023) |  10.1109/ACCESS.2023.3317434 |  This paper presents "Pera Swarm," an open-source, distributed, modular mixed-reality (MR) framework for swarm robotics experiments. It bridges the gap between pure virtual simulators (which lack real-world physics) and expensive physical swarm setups. Physical robots are tracked via an overhead camera and AR markers, while virtual robots use mathematical dead reckoning. A central simulator acts as an integrator, handling cross-reality sensing (e.g., a physical robot sensing a virtual obstacle), environment setup, and inter-agent communication. The framework was validated through performance tests measuring collision rates, sensing delays, communication latency, and scalability. |  Dead Reckoning for virtual robot localization.2D Euclidean geometry and trigonometry for line-of-sight obstacle detection using heading angles (θ, γ1, γ2).Line intersection equations to find collision points (x\_H, y\_H).Euclidean distance formulas ( d=(x2​−x1​)2+(y2​−y1​)2​ ) for proximity sensing and communication radius modeling. |  MQTT (Pub/Sub protocol for distributed communication)OpenCV2 & ARMarker libraries (for overhead camera tracking)Node.js (asynchronous event-driven runtime for the simulator)Java (for virtual robot instances)C++ (for physical robot firmware) |  The "Pera Swarm" framework running 5 physical differential-drive robots (tracked by an overhead camera) interacting concurrently with 5 virtual robot instances. The central simulator processes cross-reality distance sensing and handles message broadcasting between physical and virtual agents. |  1\. Highly cost-effective compared to deploying large physical swarms.2\. Allows testing of swarm algorithms with mixed physical-virtual populations.3\. Highly scalable for virtual robots (tested up to hundreds of instances).4\. Open-source, modular design allows easy addition of virtual sensors for hardware-constrained physical robots. |  1\. Vision-based tracking introduces localization delays, leading to higher collision rates between physical-virtual pairs.2\. Physical robots suffer from real-world physics (inertia, motor delays) not inherently mirrored by virtual robots.3\. Network/MQTT latency impacts strict real-time synchronization requirements.4\. Physical experimentation scale is bottlenecked by available hardware. |
|  6 |  Distributed Task Scheduling for MEC-Assisted Virtual Reality: A Fully-Cooperative Multiagent Perspective |  IEEE Transactions on Vehicular Technology (2024) |  10.1109/TVT.2024.3365476 |  The paper proposes a fully-cooperative distributed task scheduling scheme for Multi-access Edge Computing (MEC) assisted wireless VR. Unlike traditional self-interested models where edge servers optimize only for themselves, this approach treats the edge network as a fully-cooperative Multi-Agent System (MAS). It models the random arrival and departure of VR tasks as a "Simple Death with Immigration" process and uses Virtual Machines (VMs) for parallel multitasking. The optimization problem aims to maximize delay-satisfied tasks and load balance, formulated as a Decentralized Partially Observable Markov Decision Process (Dec-POMDP). It is solved using a Multi-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization (MAPPO) algorithm, demonstrating superior offloading success rates and load balancing compared to self-interested baselines. |  Poisson Distribution & Simple Death with Immigration (for task arrival/leaving modeling).Gaussian Distribution (for heterogeneous VR task attributes).Dec-POMDP formulation.Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) loss functions (clipped surrogate objective).Generalized Advantage Estimation (GAE). |  PyTorch 1.5.1Python 3.7PyCharm CEMulti-Agent Deep Reinforcement Learning (MAPPO algorithm) |  A simulated 6G edge network comprising 3 Base Stations (each with a MEC server). VR users with heterogeneous rendering tasks randomly arrive/leave. BSs act as cooperative agents making distributed offloading decisions via MAPPO to ensure rendering delay stays below the 20ms MTP latency threshold while balancing VM loads across the network. |  1\. Achieves significantly better network-wide QoE and load balance than self-interested models (DPPO/PPO).2\. Highly robust execution with minimal variance across different random seeds.3\. Avoids single points of failure through fully distributed execution.4\. Handles the dynamics of users randomly terminating VR sessions. |  1\. Requires a centralized training phase (CTDE) where agents must share local observations, which consumes bandwidth.2\. Training convergence takes time (approx. 70k steps).3\. Assumes VMs are proactively created, which could lead to resource waste during low-traffic periods.4\. Ignores propagation and queuing delays for simplicity.  |
|  7 |  A review of the applications of multi-agent reinforcement learning in smart factories |  Frontiers in Robotics and AI (2022) |  10.3389/frobt.2022.1027340 |  This is a comprehensive review study analyzing how Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) can solve control and decision-making challenges in Industry 4.0 smart factories. It maps smart factory attributes (uncertainty, decentralization, real-time demands) to MARL capabilities. The paper extensively reviews literature on scheduling (e.g., Job-Shop Scheduling Problems) and transportation (e.g., AGVs, UAVs), comparing centralized vs. decentralized approaches. It categorizes algorithms like Independent Q-Learning (IQL), MADDPG, and QMIX, concluding that decentralized MARL (specifically using Centralized Training and Decentralized Execution \- CTDE) is the most effective paradigm for realizing self-organizing, agile manufacturing systems. |  Markov Decision Processes (MDP) & Partially Observable MDPs (POMDP).Stochastic Games.Q-Learning and Bellman Equations.Deep Q-Networks (DQN) target network updates.Actor-Critic gradient calculations.QMIX monotonicity constraints (Individual-Global Max principle). |  Deep Neural Networks (DNNs)CNNs & LSTMs (referenced in reviewed literature for state representation)Standard RL frameworks (implied for DQN, MADDPG, QMIX) |  N/A (Review Paper).Conceptual MVP: A smart factory environment modeled as a Dec-POMDP where machines, robotic arms, and AGVs act as decentralized agents. They use CTDE algorithms like QMIX to collaboratively optimize job scheduling and pathfinding without relying on a central controller. |  1\. Provides a clear, structured mapping between complex manufacturing needs and specific MARL features.2\. Highlights the superiority of MARL over traditional heuristics (like FIFO/SPT) in dynamic environments.3\. Thoroughly categorizes both centralized and decentralized MARL approaches in manufacturing. |  1\. Being a review, it does not propose a novel technical solution or architecture.2\. Highlights inherent MARL flaws like "non-stationarity" and the "credit assignment problem" which remain unsolved challenges in the field.3\. Notes a heavy reliance on simulated environments in the literature, with a lack of real-world hardware deployment. |
|  8 |  The impact of virtual agents’ multimodal communication on brain activity and cognitive load in Virtual Reality |  Frontiers in Virtual Reality (2022) |  10.3389/frvir.2022.995090 |  This original research investigates how different interaction methods of Intelligent Virtual Agents (IVAs) in VR affect users' brain activity and cognitive load. A pilot study used a VR desert survival game where users collaborated with three agent types: Voice-only, Embodied (speech \+ gaze), and Gestural (speech \+ gaze \+ pointing). Users wore EEG caps to measure brain signals. While overall alpha band power showed no significant difference, Event-Related Desynchronization/Synchronization (ERD/ERS) analysis revealed that sudden speech without a body causes abrupt attentional demands. Conversely, adding a pointing gesture also spikes immediate attentional demand, even though subjective questionnaires showed gestures lowered overall workload and increased the feeling of "copresence." |  EEG Signal Processing: Bandpass filtering (1-40 Hz), Independent Component Analysis (ICA) for artifact removal.Alpha band power calculation.ERD/ERS formula: (Baseline \- Test) / Baseline \* 100%.Statistical analysis: Shapiro-Wilk, Friedman test, Kendall's W, Wilcoxon signed-rank with Bonferroni correction. |  HTC VIVE Pro EyeNeuracle NSW364 EEG cap (64-channel, 1000Hz)Neuracle EEGRecorder V2.0.1Unity Game EngineIBM Watson Speech to Text/Text to SpeechReady PlayerMe (3D avatar generation) |  A VR desert survival game where a user ranks 15 items. An IVA (Voice-only, Embodied, or Gestural) provides up to 10 suggestions. The user wears an EEG cap simultaneously with the VR headset to measure brain activity in the prefrontal, parietal, and occipital lobes during the collaborative task. |  1\. First study to explore the physiological impact (via EEG) of IVA interaction methods in immersive VR.2\. Combines objective physiological data (EEG) with subjective measures (NASA-TLX, SMEQ questionnaires).3\. Provides actionable design insights: matching speech with a virtual body prevents abrupt attentional spikes. |  1\. Very small sample size (11 participants, heavily male-skewed).2\. Physical friction between the EEG cap and the VR headset caused signal artifacts and electrode displacement.3\. 2-3 second delay in agent responses due to cloud API dependency.4\. No significant difference found in overall alpha band power across conditions. |
|  9 |  A review of multi-agent mobile robot systems applications |  International Journal of Electrical and Computer Engineering (IJECE), Vol. 12, No. 4, August 2022, pp. 3517\~3529 |  10.11591/ijece.v12i4.pp3517-3529 |  This paper provides a comprehensive survey of Multi-Agent Robot Systems (MARS), a fusion of Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) and Multi-Robot Systems (MRS). It categorizes MAS architectures into Centralized, Decentralized, and Federated structures. The paper differentiates between Autonomous Mobile Robots (AMRs) and Autonomous Guided Vehicles (AGVs), highlighting the cognitive superiority of AMRs. It reviews 21 recent studies (2017-2021) focusing on their motivations, contributions, and limitations in areas like SLAM, path planning, and cooperative control. Finally, the authors propose a novel real-time MARS application: a main server managing five mobile robots distributed across a five-floor building, utilizing hybrid meta-heuristic algorithms for dynamic/static obstacle avoidance and secure communications. |  Combinatorial methods, Sampling-based methods, Bio-inspired algorithms (Genetic Algorithms, Particle Swarm Optimization), Non-linear kinematics models, Bernoulli distribution (in SPSAs), Graph theory, Asymptotic exponential functions, Non-smooth barrier functions (NBFs), Simultaneous Perturbation Stochastic Approximation (SPSA). |  Robot Operating System (ROS), Virtual Robot Experimentation Platform (V-REP), Java Agent Development Framework (JADE), Cloud computing platforms, Blockchain (proposed for swarm security), Computer vision, Khepera IV robot platform, LiDAR, Range sensors, DALI (multi-agent language). |  The conceptual MVP proposed by the authors is a 5-floor building management system where 5 distinct mobile robots operate on different floors with unique map environments. It is controlled by a central main server and features an adaptive intelligent controller, secure network communication, and hybrid meta-heuristic path planning capable of handling both static and dynamic obstacles. Other MVPs discussed in the reviewed literature include cloud-based vision systems for mobile robots and multi-agent cooperative SLAM frameworks. |  1\. Higher efficiency and detailed results compared to single-robot systems.2\. Benefits of information sharing, data fusion, and distributed task allocation.3\. Lower cost and higher robustness compared to monolithic systems.4\. High adaptability, reconfigurability, and partial technology independence.5\. Highly scalable for diverse applications (aerial drones, ground warehouses, aquatic exploration, healthcare disinfection). |  1\. Highly complex coordination strategies required among robots.2\. Communication instability, time delays, and data loss between agents and base stations.3\. High power/energy consumption constraints.4\. Difficulty in real-time processing and dynamic obstacle avoidance in cluttered environments.5\. Many algorithms reviewed remain strictly in the simulation stage without real-world testing.6\. Lack of stability and convergence proofs in several proposed controllers.7\. Security vulnerabilities in wireless multi-master communication networks. |
|  10 |  Can Virtual AI Agents Improve Cognitive Assessment? A Virtual Reality Perspective |  The PErvasive Technologies Related to Assistive Environments (PETRA ’25), June 25–27, 2025, Corfu Island, Greece |  10.1145/3733155.3734917 |  This paper introduces "SmartSage," an intelligent virtual AI agent designed to enhance cognitive assessment, specifically for ADHD, by integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision Language Models (VLMs) within a Virtual Reality (VR) environment. Built on the clinically validated Automated Test of Embodied Cognition (ATEC) framework, the system uses the Meta Quest platform to track motion and provide haptic feedback. SmartSage guides users through two physical tasks—Pass the Ball to the Beat (PBB) and Cross Your Body (CYB)—delivering real-time demonstrations, personalized feedback, and dynamically adjusting task difficulty based on user performance. A pilot study with 8 participants demonstrated that while traditional video guides (Eliza) were initially easier to understand, SmartSage provided superior feedback quality, higher engagement, and greater user confidence, indicating strong potential for accessible and ecologically valid cognitive assessments. |  Rhythmic accuracy metrics, movement precision calculations, response timing analysis, and statistical evaluation of user survey data (Likert scale agreement distributions). |  Meta Quest VR Headset and Controllers (Motion tracking & Haptics), Unity Game Engine, Large Language Models (Phi 3), Vision Language Models (PaliGemma 2), Machine Learning algorithms for real-time data analysis. |  The working prototype MVP is the SmartSage VR application itself. It features a virtual AI avatar guiding users through two distinct ATEC tasks (PBB and CYB) in a VR room, capable of interpreting visual cues, tracking physical movements via controllers, and dynamically scaling task difficulty in real-time. |  1\. Maintains clinical validity while significantly boosting user engagement through immersive VR.2\. Provides personalized, real-time adaptive feedback, unlike static video instructions.3\. Evaluates both task accuracy and rhythm accuracy, offering a more nuanced understanding of ADHD than screen-based tests.4\. High ecological validity due to the integration of embodied cognition (physical movement). |  1\. Initial task explanation phase is less effective than straightforward traditional video guides.2\. Variability in user comfort levels when interacting with VR controllers.3\. Pilot study sample size was very small (8 participants).4\. Lacks extensive clinical validation across diverse populations and neurodivergent profiles at this stage. |
|  11 |  RoboOS: A Hierarchical Embodied Framework for Cross-Embodiment and Multi-Agent Collaboration |  arXiv (Pre-print, submitted June 5, 2025\) |  arXiv:2505.03673v2 |  This paper presents RoboOS, the first open-source embodied operating system built on a biologically inspired "Brain-Cerebellum" hierarchical architecture designed to transition robotic systems from single-agent to multi-agent intelligence. It addresses limitations in current Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, such as poor long-horizon planning and lack of cross-embodiment compatibility. RoboOS consists of three core components: (1) The Embodied Brain Model (RoboBrain), a cloud-based Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) for global perception, task decomposition, and error correction; (2) The Cerebellum Skill Library, an edge-deployed modular toolkit for low-latency execution of manipulation and navigation skills across heterogeneous robots; and (3) Real-Time Shared Memory, a spatiotemporal synchronization hub for coordinating multi-agent states. Validated in real-world scenarios (restaurants, households, supermarkets) using diverse robots (humanoids, single/dual-arm, wheeled), RoboOS successfully demonstrated complex collaborative tasks like cross-embodiment burger preparation and gift packaging. |  Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) fusion formulas, Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAG) for topological subtask allocation, 3D scene graph algorithms for spatial memory, Discrete Fréchet Distance (DFD), Hausdorff Distance (HD), and RMSE for trajectory evaluation. |  Cloud-based distributed inference (FlagScale framework), Qwen2.5-VL-7B (MLLM base), OpenVLA, RDT-1B, π0 (VLA tools), SLAM, Vision-Language-Navigation (MapNav, NavID), SAM segmentation, CLIP features, Redis (Shared Memory), Edge-Cloud publish-subscribe architecture. |  The MVP involves real-world demonstrations in three scenarios: (1) A restaurant where a Unitree G1 humanoid and AgileX dual-arm robot collaborate to make and deliver a burger; (2) A household where Realman single-arm and AgileX dual-arm robots fetch an orange and knife; (3) A supermarket where robots coordinate to select and package a gift into a bag. |  1\. Achieves true cross-embodiment compatibility, allowing completely different robots to collaborate seamlessly.2\. Overcomes the long-horizon planning limitations of end-to-end VLA models through hierarchical task decomposition.3\. Features dynamic error correction and real-time replanning via continuous feedback loops.4\. Ultra-low latency edge-cloud communication (command response \< 0.001s). |  1\. Current real-world experiments are limited to restaurants, households, and supermarkets; untested in complex industrial settings (e.g., assembly lines, logistics hubs).2\. High computational overhead required for cloud-based RoboBrain inference.3\. Dependency on accurate real-time 3D scene graph construction, which can be challenging in highly unstructured or occluded environments. |
|  12 |  Applying Theory of Mind to Multi-Agent Systems: A Systematic Review |  N/A (Systematic Review Paper, likely conference proceeding/journal based on formatting and references) |  N/A (Not provided in text) |  This paper presents a systematic literature review following PRISMA guidelines to analyze how the cognitive science concept of "Theory of Mind" (ToM)—the ability to attribute mental states like beliefs, desires, and intentions to others—has been applied to Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) between 2002 and 2022\. Out of 698 initially identified papers, 60 were selected for deep analysis. The review answers 9 research questions, revealing that most MAS research focuses on modeling standard BDI (Beliefs-Desires-Intentions) architectures using formal logic representations. While applications span games, negotiations, and human-agent interaction, most works remain theoretical proofs-of-concept. The paper identifies significant challenges, including handling the dynamism of real-world environments, scaling ToM inferences, and capturing implicit beliefs. It concludes by mapping cognitive ToM theories (Theory-Theory, Simulation Theory) to AI paradigms (Symbolic, Subsymbolic, Neurosymbolic), highlighting a gap in achieving realistic, human-like mentalization in agent-agent interactions. |  Formal representations including Epistemic Logic, First-Order Logic (FOL), Propositional Logic, Bayesian Modeling, Markov Decision Processes (MDMs), and POMDPs. |  Agent-Oriented Programming Languages (JaCaMo, 2APL, JADE), Machine Learning (Neural Networks, Deep Learning, LSTMs), Probabilistic frameworks, 2D/3D Simulation Platforms, Robotics middleware (ROS), Ontologies (Ontologenius). |  N/A \- This is a review paper. However, the most common proof-of-concept MVPs identified in the reviewed literature include: (1) Virtual agents in games (e.g., Hanabi) using ToM to outperform non-ToM agents; (2) Personal assistant chatbots modeling human user beliefs to improve interaction; (3) Decision-theoretic agents in simulated social dilemmas (e.g., Disaster World, PsychSim). |  1\. Provides a comprehensive, structured mapping of 20 years of research at the intersection of cognitive science and MAS.2\. Clearly identifies the dominant paradigms (BDI, formal logic) and maps them to cognitive theories.3\. Highlights critical research gaps, such as the lack of neurosymbolic (Hybrid Theory) implementations in MAS.4\. Establishes a clear agenda for developing more human-like social intelligence in AI. |  1\. More than half of the reviewed papers failed to explicitly report their limitations or challenges.2\. The majority of the 60 reviewed works are theoretical or rely on highly controlled simulations, lacking real-world validation.3\. Many empirical studies suffered from small sample sizes (both in terms of human participants and agent populations).4\. Current mathematical and logical frameworks are noted as inadequate for fully capturing the nuances of human ToM. |

|  14 |  RoboOS: A Hierarchical Embodied Framework for Cross-Embodiment and Multi-Agent Collaboration |  arXiv (Pre-print, submitted June 5, 2025\) |  arXiv:2505.03673v2 |  This paper presents RoboOS, the first open-source embodied operating system built on a biologically inspired "Brain-Cerebellum" hierarchical architecture designed to transition robotic systems from single-agent to multi-agent intelligence. It addresses limitations in current Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, such as poor long-horizon planning and lack of cross-embodiment compatibility. RoboOS consists of three core components: (1) The Embodied Brain Model (RoboBrain), a cloud-based Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) for global perception, task decomposition, and error correction; (2) The Cerebellum Skill Library, an edge-deployed modular toolkit for low-latency execution of manipulation and navigation skills across heterogeneous robots; and (3) Real-Time Shared Memory, a spatiotemporal synchronization hub for coordinating multi-agent states. Validated in real-world scenarios (restaurants, households, supermarkets) using diverse robots (humanoids, single/dual-arm, wheeled), RoboOS successfully demonstrated complex collaborative tasks like cross-embodiment burger preparation and gift packaging. |  Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) fusion formulas, Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAG) for topological subtask allocation, 3D scene graph algorithms for spatial memory, Discrete Fréchet Distance (DFD), Hausdorff Distance (HD), and RMSE for trajectory evaluation. |  Cloud-based distributed inference (FlagScale framework), Qwen2.5-VL-7B (MLLM base), OpenVLA, RDT-1B, π0 (VLA tools), SLAM, Vision-Language-Navigation (MapNav, NavID), SAM segmentation, CLIP features, Redis (Shared Memory), Edge-Cloud publish-subscribe architecture. |  The MVP involves real-world demonstrations in three scenarios: (1) A restaurant where a Unitree G1 humanoid and AgileX dual-arm robot collaborate to make and deliver a burger; (2) A household where Realman single-arm and AgileX dual-arm robots fetch an orange and knife; (3) A supermarket where robots coordinate to select and package a gift into a bag. |  1\. Achieves true cross-embodiment compatibility, allowing completely different robots to collaborate seamlessly.2\. Overcomes the long-horizon planning limitations of end-to-end VLA models through hierarchical task decomposition.3\. Features dynamic error correction and real-time replanning via continuous feedback loops.4\. Ultra-low latency edge-cloud communication (command response \< 0.001s). |  1\. Current real-world experiments are limited to restaurants, households, and supermarkets; untested in complex industrial settings (e.g., assembly lines, logistics hubs).2\. High computational overhead required for cloud-based RoboBrain inference.3\. Dependency on accurate real-time 3D scene graph construction, which can be challenging in highly unstructured or occluded environments. |
| 15 | Applying Theory of Mind to Multi-Agent Systems: A Systematic Review | N/A (Systematic Review Paper, likely conference proceeding/journal based on formatting and references) | N/A (Not provided in text) | This paper presents a systematic literature review following PRISMA guidelines to analyze how the cognitive science concept of "Theory of Mind" (ToM)—the ability to attribute mental states like beliefs, desires, and intentions to others—has been applied to Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) between 2002 and 2022\. Out of 698 initially identified papers, 60 were selected for deep analysis. The review answers 9 research questions, revealing that most MAS research focuses on modeling standard BDI (Beliefs-Desires-Intentions) architectures using formal logic representations. While applications span games, negotiations, and human-agent interaction, most works remain theoretical proofs-of-concept. The paper identifies significant challenges, including handling the dynamism of real-world environments, scaling ToM inferences, and capturing implicit beliefs. It concludes by mapping cognitive ToM theories (Theory-Theory, Simulation Theory) to AI paradigms (Symbolic, Subsymbolic, Neurosymbolic), highlighting a gap in achieving realistic, human-like mentalization in agent-agent interactions. | Formal representations including Epistemic Logic, First-Order Logic (FOL), Propositional Logic, Bayesian Modeling, Markov Decision Processes (MDMs), and POMDPs. | Agent-Oriented Programming Languages (JaCaMo, 2APL, JADE), Machine Learning (Neural Networks, Deep Learning, LSTMs), Probabilistic frameworks, 2D/3D Simulation Platforms, Robotics middleware (ROS), Ontologies (Ontologenius). | N/A \- This is a review paper. However, the most common proof-of-concept MVPs identified in the reviewed literature include: (1) Virtual agents in games (e.g., Hanabi) using ToM to outperform non-ToM agents; (2) Personal assistant chatbots modeling human user beliefs to improve interaction; (3) Decision-theoretic agents in simulated social dilemmas (e.g., Disaster World, PsychSim). | 1\. Provides a comprehensive, structured mapping of 20 years of research at the intersection of cognitive science and MAS.2\. Clearly identifies the dominant paradigms (BDI, formal logic) and maps them to cognitive theories.3\. Highlights critical research gaps, such as the lack of neurosymbolic (Hybrid Theory) implementations in MAS.4\. Establishes a clear agenda for developing more human-like social intelligence in AI. | 1\. More than half of the reviewed papers failed to explicitly report their limitations or challenges.2\. The majority of the 60 reviewed works are theoretical or rely on highly controlled simulations, lacking real-world validation.3\. Many empirical studies suffered from small sample sizes (both in terms of human participants and agent populations).4\. Current mathematical and logical frameworks are noted as inadequate for fully capturing the nuances of human ToM. |
| Sr. No. | Name of the paper | Publication | Doi | Detailed Summary | Mathematics used | Technology stack used | MVP of it | Advantages | Disadvantages |
| 1 | Can Virtual AI Agents Improve Cognitive Assessment? A Virtual Reality Perspective | The PErvasive Technologies Related to Assistive Environments (PETRA ’25), June 25–27, 2025, Corfu Island, Greece | 10.1145/3733155.3734917 | This paper introduces "SmartSage," an intelligent virtual AI agent designed to enhance cognitive assessment, specifically for ADHD, by integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision Language Models (VLMs) within a Virtual Reality (VR) environment. Built on the clinically validated Automated Test of Embodied Cognition (ATEC) framework, the system uses the Meta Quest platform to track motion and provide haptic feedback. SmartSage guides users through two physical tasks—Pass the Ball to the Beat (PBB) and Cross Your Body (CYB)—delivering real-time demonstrations, personalized feedback, and dynamically adjusting task difficulty based on user performance. A pilot study with 8 participants demonstrated that while traditional video guides (Eliza) were initially easier to understand, SmartSage provided superior feedback quality, higher engagement, and greater user confidence, indicating strong potential for accessible and ecologically valid cognitive assessments. | Rhythmic accuracy metrics, movement precision calculations, response timing analysis, and statistical evaluation of user survey data (Likert scale agreement distributions). | Meta Quest VR Headset and Controllers (Motion tracking & Haptics), Unity Game Engine, Large Language Models (Phi 3), Vision Language Models (PaliGemma 2), Machine Learning algorithms for real-time data analysis. | The working prototype MVP is the SmartSage VR application itself. It features a virtual AI avatar guiding users through two distinct ATEC tasks (PBB and CYB) in a VR room, capable of interpreting visual cues, tracking physical movements via controllers, and dynamically scaling task difficulty in real-time. | 1\. Maintains clinical validity while significantly boosting user engagement through immersive VR.2\. Provides personalized, real-time adaptive feedback, unlike static video instructions.3\. Evaluates both task accuracy and rhythm accuracy, offering a more nuanced understanding of ADHD than screen-based tests.4\. High ecological validity due to the integration of embodied cognition (physical movement). | 1\. Initial task explanation phase is less effective than straightforward traditional video guides.2\. Variability in user comfort levels when interacting with VR controllers.3\. Pilot study sample size was very small (8 participants).4\. Lacks extensive clinical validation across diverse populations and neurodivergent profiles at this stage. |

Validated in real-world scenarios (restaurants, households, supermarkets) using diverse robots (humanoids, single/dual-arm, wheeled), RoboOS successfully demonstrated complex collaborative tasks like cross-embodiment burger preparation and gift packaging. | Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) fusion formulas, Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAG) for topological subtask allocation, 3D scene graph algorithms for spatial memory, Discrete Fréchet Distance (DFD), Hausdorff Distance (HD), and RMSE for trajectory evaluation. | Cloud-based distributed inference (FlagScale framework), Qwen2.5-VL-7B (MLLM base), OpenVLA, RDT-1B, π0 (VLA tools), SLAM, Vision-Language-Navigation (MapNav, NavID), SAM segmentation, CLIP features, Redis (Shared Memory), Edge-Cloud publish-subscribe architecture. | The MVP involves real-world demonstrations in three scenarios: (1) A restaurant where a Unitree G1 humanoid and AgileX dual-arm robot collaborate to make and deliver a burger; (2) A household where Realman single-arm and AgileX dual-arm robots fetch an orange and knife; (3) A supermarket where robots coordinate to select and package a gift into a bag. | 1\. Achieves true cross-embodiment compatibility, allowing completely different robots to collaborate seamlessly.2\. Overcomes the long-horizon planning limitations of end-to-end VLA models through hierarchical task decomposition.3\. Features dynamic error correction and real-time replanning via continuous feedback loops.4\. Ultra-low latency edge-cloud communication (command response \< 0.001s). | 1\. Current real-world experiments are limited to restaurants, households, and supermarkets; untested in complex industrial settings (e.g., assembly lines, logistics hubs).2\. High computational overhead required for cloud-based RoboBrain inference.3\. Dependency on accurate real-time 3D scene graph construction, which can be challenging in highly unstructured or occluded environments. |
| 3 | Applying Theory of Mind to Multi-Agent Systems: A Systematic Review | N/A (Systematic Review Paper, likely conference proceeding/journal based on formatting and references) | N/A (Not provided in text) | This paper presents a systematic literature review following PRISMA guidelines to analyze how the cognitive science concept of "Theory of Mind" (ToM)—the ability to attribute mental states like beliefs, desires, and intentions to others—has been applied to Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) between 2002 and 2022\. Out of 698 initially identified papers, 60 were selected for deep analysis. The review answers 9 research questions, revealing that most MAS research focuses on modeling standard BDI (Beliefs-Desires-Intentions) architectures using formal logic representations. While applications span games, negotiations, and human-agent interaction, most works remain theoretical proofs-of-concept. The paper identifies significant challenges, including handling the dynamism of real-world environments, scaling ToM inferences, and capturing implicit beliefs. It concludes by mapping cognitive ToM theories (Theory-Theory, Simulation Theory) to AI paradigms (Symbolic, Subsymbolic, Neurosymbolic), highlighting a gap in achieving realistic, human-like mentalization in agent-agent interactions. | Formal representations including Epistemic Logic, First-Order Logic (FOL), Propositional Logic, Bayesian Modeling, Markov Decision Processes (MDMs), and POMDPs. | Agent-Oriented Programming Languages (JaCaMo, 2APL, JADE), Machine Learning (Neural Networks, Deep Learning, LSTMs), Probabilistic frameworks, 2D/3D Simulation Platforms, Robotics middleware (ROS), Ontologies (Ontologenius). | N/A \- This is a review paper. However, the most common proof-of-concept MVPs identified in the reviewed literature include: (1) Virtual agents in games (e.g., Hanabi) using ToM to outperform non-ToM agents; (2) Personal assistant chatbots modeling human user beliefs to improve interaction; (3) Decision-theoretic agents in simulated social dilemmas (e.g., Disaster World, PsychSim). | 1\. Provides a comprehensive, structured mapping of 20 years of research at the intersection of cognitive science and MAS.2\. Clearly identifies the dominant paradigms (BDI, formal logic) and maps them to cognitive theories.3\. Highlights critical research gaps, such as the lack of neurosymbolic (Hybrid Theory) implementations in MAS.4\. Establishes a clear agenda for developing more human-like social intelligence in AI. | 1\. More than half of the reviewed papers failed to explicitly report their limitations or challenges.2\. The majority of the 60 reviewed works are theoretical or rely on highly controlled simulations, lacking real-world validation.3\. Many empirical studies suffered from small sample sizes (both in terms of human participants and agent populations).4\. Current mathematical and logical frameworks are noted as inadequate for fully capturing the nuances of human ToM. |
| Sr. No. | Name of the paper | Publication | Doi | Detailed Summary | Mathematics used | Technology stack used | MVP of it | Advantages | Disadvantages |
| 1 | Can Virtual AI Agents Improve Cognitive Assessment? A Virtual Reality Perspective | The PErvasive Technologies Related to Assistive Environments (PETRA ’25), June 25–27, 2025, Corfu Island, Greece | 10.1145/3733155.3734917 | This paper introduces "SmartSage," an intelligent virtual AI agent designed to enhance cognitive assessment, specifically for ADHD, by integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision Language Models (VLMs) within a Virtual Reality (VR) environment. Built on the clinically validated Automated Test of Embodied Cognition (ATEC) framework, the system uses the Meta Quest platform to track motion and provide haptic feedback. SmartSage guides users through two physical tasks—Pass the Ball to the Beat (PBB) and Cross Your Body (CYB)—delivering real-time demonstrations, personalized feedback, and dynamically adjusting task difficulty based on user performance. A pilot study with 8 participants demonstrated that while traditional video guides (Eliza) were initially easier to understand, SmartSage provided superior feedback quality, higher engagement, and greater user confidence, indicating strong potential for accessible and ecologically valid cognitive assessments. | Rhythmic accuracy metrics, movement precision calculations, response timing analysis, and statistical evaluation of user survey data (Likert scale agreement distributions). | Meta Quest VR Headset and Controllers (Motion tracking & Haptics), Unity Game Engine, Large Language Models (Phi 3), Vision Language Models (PaliGemma 2), Machine Learning algorithms for real-time data analysis. | The working prototype MVP is the SmartSage VR application itself. It features a virtual AI avatar guiding users through two distinct ATEC tasks (PBB and CYB) in a VR room, capable of interpreting visual cues, tracking physical movements via controllers, and dynamically scaling task difficulty in real-time. | 1\. Maintains clinical validity while significantly boosting user engagement through immersive VR.2\. Provides personalized, real-time adaptive feedback, unlike static video instructions.3\. Evaluates both task accuracy and rhythm accuracy, offering a more nuanced understanding of ADHD than screen-based tests.4\. High ecological validity due to the integration of embodied cognition (physical movement). | 1\. Initial task explanation phase is less effective than straightforward traditional video guides.2\. Variability in user comfort levels when interacting with VR controllers.3\. Pilot study sample size was very small (8 participants).4\. Lacks extensive clinical validation across diverse populations and neurodivergent profiles at this stage. |

Validated in real-world scenarios (restaurants, households, supermarkets) using diverse robots (humanoids, single/dual-arm, wheeled), RoboOS successfully demonstrated complex collaborative tasks like cross-embodiment burger preparation and gift packaging. | Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) fusion formulas, Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAG) for topological subtask allocation, 3D scene graph algorithms for spatial memory, Discrete Fréchet Distance (DFD), Hausdorff Distance (HD), and RMSE for trajectory evaluation. | Cloud-based distributed inference (FlagScale framework), Qwen2.5-VL-7B (MLLM base), OpenVLA, RDT-1B, π0 (VLA tools), SLAM, Vision-Language-Navigation (MapNav, NavID), SAM segmentation, CLIP features, Redis (Shared Memory), Edge-Cloud publish-subscribe architecture. | The MVP involves real-world demonstrations in three scenarios: (1) A restaurant where a Unitree G1 humanoid and AgileX dual-arm robot collaborate to make and deliver a burger; (2) A household where Realman single-arm and AgileX dual-arm robots fetch an orange and knife; (3) A supermarket where robots coordinate to select and package a gift into a bag. | 1\. Achieves true cross-embodiment compatibility, allowing completely different robots to collaborate seamlessly.2\. Overcomes the long-horizon planning limitations of end-to-end VLA models through hierarchical task decomposition.3\. Features dynamic error correction and real-time replanning via continuous feedback loops.4\. Ultra-low latency edge-cloud communication (command response \< 0.001s). | 1\. Current real-world experiments are limited to restaurants, households, and supermarkets; untested in complex industrial settings (e.g., assembly lines, logistics hubs).2\. High computational overhead required for cloud-based RoboBrain inference.3\. Dependency on accurate real-time 3D scene graph construction, which can be challenging in highly unstructured or occluded environments. |
| 3 | Applying Theory of Mind to Multi-Agent Systems: A Systematic Review | N/A (Systematic Review Paper, likely conference proceeding/journal based on formatting and references) | N/A (Not provided in text) | This paper presents a systematic literature review following PRISMA guidelines to analyze how the cognitive science concept of "Theory of Mind" (ToM)—the ability to attribute mental states like beliefs, desires, and intentions to others—has been applied to Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) between 2002 and 2022\. Out of 698 initially identified papers, 60 were selected for deep analysis. The review answers 9 research questions, revealing that most MAS research focuses on modeling standard BDI (Beliefs-Desires-Intentions) architectures using formal logic representations. While applications span games, negotiations, and human-agent interaction, most works remain theoretical proofs-of-concept. The paper identifies significant challenges, including handling the dynamism of real-world environments, scaling ToM inferences, and capturing implicit beliefs. It concludes by mapping cognitive ToM theories (Theory-Theory, Simulation Theory) to AI paradigms (Symbolic, Subsymbolic, Neurosymbolic), highlighting a gap in achieving realistic, human-like mentalization in agent-agent interactions. | Formal representations including Epistemic Logic, First-Order Logic (FOL), Propositional Logic, Bayesian Modeling, Markov Decision Processes (MDMs), and POMDPs. | Agent-Oriented Programming Languages (JaCaMo, 2APL, JADE), Machine Learning (Neural Networks, Deep Learning, LSTMs), Probabilistic frameworks, 2D/3D Simulation Platforms, Robotics middleware (ROS), Ontologies (Ontologenius). | N/A \- This is a review paper. However, the most common proof-of-concept MVPs identified in the reviewed literature include: (1) Virtual agents in games (e.g., Hanabi) using ToM to outperform non-ToM agents; (2) Personal assistant chatbots modeling human user beliefs to improve interaction; (3) Decision-theoretic agents in simulated social dilemmas (e.g., Disaster World, PsychSim). | 1\. Provides a comprehensive, structured mapping of 20 years of research at the intersection of cognitive science and MAS.2\. Clearly identifies the dominant paradigms (BDI, formal logic) and maps them to cognitive theories.3\. Highlights critical research gaps, such as the lack of neurosymbolic (Hybrid Theory) implementations in MAS.4\. Establishes a clear agenda for developing more human-like social intelligence in AI. | 1\. More than half of the reviewed papers failed to explicitly report their limitations or challenges.2\. The majority of the 60 reviewed works are theoretical or rely on highly controlled simulations, lacking real-world validation.3\. Many empirical studies suffered from small sample sizes (both in terms of human participants and agent populations).4\. Current mathematical and logical frameworks are noted as inadequate for fully capturing the nuances of human ToM. |
| Sr. No. | Name of the paper | Publication | Doi | Detailed Summary | Mathematics used | Technology stack used | MVP of it | Advantages | Disadvantages |
| 1 | Can Virtual AI Agents Improve Cognitive Assessment? A Virtual Reality Perspective | The PErvasive Technologies Related to Assistive Environments (PETRA ’25), June 25–27, 2025, Corfu Island, Greece | 10.1145/3733155.3734917 | This paper introduces "SmartSage," an intelligent virtual AI agent designed to enhance cognitive assessment, specifically for ADHD, by integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision Language Models (VLMs) within a Virtual Reality (VR) environment. Built on the clinically validated Automated Test of Embodied Cognition (ATEC) framework, the system uses the Meta Quest platform to track motion and provide haptic feedback. SmartSage guides users through two physical tasks—Pass the Ball to the Beat (PBB) and Cross Your Body (CYB)—delivering real-time demonstrations, personalized feedback, and dynamically adjusting task difficulty based on user performance. A pilot study with 8 participants demonstrated that while traditional video guides (Eliza) were initially easier to understand, SmartSage provided superior feedback quality, higher engagement, and greater user confidence, indicating strong potential for accessible and ecologically valid cognitive assessments. | Rhythmic accuracy metrics, movement precision calculations, response timing analysis, and statistical evaluation of user survey data (Likert scale agreement distributions). | Meta Quest VR Headset and Controllers (Motion tracking & Haptics), Unity Game Engine, Large Language Models (Phi 3), Vision Language Models (PaliGemma 2), Machine Learning algorithms for real-time data analysis. | The working prototype MVP is the SmartSage VR application itself. It features a virtual AI avatar guiding users through two distinct ATEC tasks (PBB and CYB) in a VR room, capable of interpreting visual cues, tracking physical movements via controllers, and dynamically scaling task difficulty in real-time. | 1\. Maintains clinical validity while significantly boosting user engagement through immersive VR.2\. Provides personalized, real-time adaptive feedback, unlike static video instructions.3\. Evaluates both task accuracy and rhythm accuracy, offering a more nuanced understanding of ADHD than screen-based tests.4\. High ecological validity due to the integration of embodied cognition (physical movement). | 1\. Initial task explanation phase is less effective than straightforward traditional video guides.2\. Variability in user comfort levels when interacting with VR controllers.3\. Pilot study sample size was very small (8 participants).4\. Lacks extensive clinical validation across diverse populations and neurodivergent profiles at this stage. |

Validated in real-world scenarios (restaurants, households, supermarkets) using diverse robots (humanoids, single/dual-arm, wheeled), RoboOS successfully demonstrated complex collaborative tasks like cross-embodiment burger preparation and gift packaging. | Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) fusion formulas, Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAG) for topological subtask allocation, 3D scene graph algorithms for spatial memory, Discrete Fréchet Distance (DFD), Hausdorff Distance (HD), and RMSE for trajectory evaluation. | Cloud-based distributed inference (FlagScale framework), Qwen2.5-VL-7B (MLLM base), OpenVLA, RDT-1B, π0 (VLA tools), SLAM, Vision-Language-Navigation (MapNav, NavID), SAM segmentation, CLIP features, Redis (Shared Memory), Edge-Cloud publish-subscribe architecture. | The MVP involves real-world demonstrations in three scenarios: (1) A restaurant where a Unitree G1 humanoid and AgileX dual-arm robot collaborate to make and deliver a burger; (2) A household where Realman single-arm and AgileX dual-arm robots fetch an orange and knife; (3) A supermarket where robots coordinate to select and package a gift into a bag. | 1\. Achieves true cross-embodiment compatibility, allowing completely different robots to collaborate seamlessly.2\. Overcomes the long-horizon planning limitations of end-to-end VLA models through hierarchical task decomposition.3\. Features dynamic error correction and real-time replanning via continuous feedback loops.4\. Ultra-low latency edge-cloud communication (command response \< 0.001s). | 1\. Current real-world experiments are limited to restaurants, households, and supermarkets; untested in complex industrial settings (e.g., assembly lines, logistics hubs).2\. High computational overhead required for cloud-based RoboBrain inference.3\. Dependency on accurate real-time 3D scene graph construction, which can be challenging in highly unstructured or occluded environments. |
| 3 | Applying Theory of Mind to Multi-Agent Systems: A Systematic Review | N/A (Systematic Review Paper, likely conference proceeding/journal based on formatting and references) | N/A (Not provided in text) | This paper presents a systematic literature review following PRISMA guidelines to analyze how the cognitive science concept of "Theory of Mind" (ToM)—the ability to attribute mental states like beliefs, desires, and intentions to others—has been applied to Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) between 2002 and 2022\. Out of 698 initially identified papers, 60 were selected for deep analysis. The review answers 9 research questions, revealing that most MAS research focuses on modeling standard BDI (Beliefs-Desires-Intentions) architectures using formal logic representations. While applications span games, negotiations, and human-agent interaction, most works remain theoretical proofs-of-concept. The paper identifies significant challenges, including handling the dynamism of real-world environments, scaling ToM inferences, and capturing implicit beliefs. It concludes by mapping cognitive ToM theories (Theory-Theory, Simulation Theory) to AI paradigms (Symbolic, Subsymbolic, Neurosymbolic), highlighting a gap in achieving realistic, human-like mentalization in agent-agent interactions. | Formal representations including Epistemic Logic, First-Order Logic (FOL), Propositional Logic, Bayesian Modeling, Markov Decision Processes (MDMs), and POMDPs. | Agent-Oriented Programming Languages (JaCaMo, 2APL, JADE), Machine Learning (Neural Networks, Deep Learning, LSTMs), Probabilistic frameworks, 2D/3D Simulation Platforms, Robotics middleware (ROS), Ontologies (Ontologenius). | N/A \- This is a review paper. However, the most common proof-of-concept MVPs identified in the reviewed literature include: (1) Virtual agents in games (e.g., Hanabi) using ToM to outperform non-ToM agents; (2) Personal assistant chatbots modeling human user beliefs to improve interaction; (3) Decision-theoretic agents in simulated social dilemmas (e.g., Disaster World, PsychSim). | 1\. Provides a comprehensive, structured mapping of 20 years of research at the intersection of cognitive science and MAS.2\. Clearly identifies the dominant paradigms (BDI, formal logic) and maps them to cognitive theories.3\. Highlights critical research gaps, such as the lack of neurosymbolic (Hybrid Theory) implementations in MAS.4\. Establishes a clear agenda for developing more human-like social intelligence in AI. | 1\. More than half of the reviewed papers failed to explicitly report their limitations or challenges.2\. The majority of the 60 reviewed works are theoretical or rely on highly controlled simulations, lacking real-world validation.3\. Many empirical studies suffered from small sample sizes (both in terms of human participants and agent populations).4\. Current mathematical and logical frameworks are noted as inadequate for fully capturing the nuances of human ToM. |
| Sr. No. | Name of the paper | Publication | Doi | Detailed Summary | Mathematics used | Technology stack used | MVP of it | Advantages | Disadvantages |
| 1 | Can Virtual AI Agents Improve Cognitive Assessment? A Virtual Reality Perspective | The PErvasive Technologies Related to Assistive Environments (PETRA ’25), June 25–27, 2025, Corfu Island, Greece | 10.1145/3733155.3734917 | This paper introduces "SmartSage," an intelligent virtual AI agent designed to enhance cognitive assessment, specifically for ADHD, by integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision Language Models (VLMs) within a Virtual Reality (VR) environment. Built on the clinically validated Automated Test of Embodied Cognition (ATEC) framework, the system uses the Meta Quest platform to track motion and provide haptic feedback. SmartSage guides users through two physical tasks—Pass the Ball to the Beat (PBB) and Cross Your Body (CYB)—delivering real-time demonstrations, personalized feedback, and dynamically adjusting task difficulty based on user performance. A pilot study with 8 participants demonstrated that while traditional video guides (Eliza) were initially easier to understand, SmartSage provided superior feedback quality, higher engagement, and greater user confidence, indicating strong potential for accessible and ecologically valid cognitive assessments. | Rhythmic accuracy metrics, movement precision calculations, response timing analysis, and statistical evaluation of user survey data (Likert scale agreement distributions). | Meta Quest VR Headset and Controllers (Motion tracking & Haptics), Unity Game Engine, Large Language Models (Phi 3), Vision Language Models (PaliGemma 2), Machine Learning algorithms for real-time data analysis. | The working prototype MVP is the SmartSage VR application itself. It features a virtual AI avatar guiding users through two distinct ATEC tasks (PBB and CYB) in a VR room, capable of interpreting visual cues, tracking physical movements via controllers, and dynamically scaling task difficulty in real-time. | 1\. Maintains clinical validity while significantly boosting user engagement through immersive VR.2\. Provides personalized, real-time adaptive feedback, unlike static video instructions.3\. Evaluates both task accuracy and rhythm accuracy, offering a more nuanced understanding of ADHD than screen-based tests.4\. High ecological validity due to the integration of embodied cognition (physical movement). | 1\. Initial task explanation phase is less effective than straightforward traditional video guides.2\. Variability in user comfort levels when interacting with VR controllers.3\. Pilot study sample size was very small (8 participants).4\. Lacks extensive clinical validation across diverse populations and neurodivergent profiles at this stage. |

Validated in real-world scenarios (restaurants, households, supermarkets) using diverse robots (humanoids, single/dual-arm, wheeled), RoboOS successfully demonstrated complex collaborative tasks like cross-embodiment burger preparation and gift packaging. | Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) fusion formulas, Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAG) for topological subtask allocation, 3D scene graph algorithms for spatial memory, Discrete Fréchet Distance (DFD), Hausdorff Distance (HD), and RMSE for trajectory evaluation. | Cloud-based distributed inference (FlagScale framework), Qwen2.5-VL-7B (MLLM base), OpenVLA, RDT-1B, π0 (VLA tools), SLAM, Vision-Language-Navigation (MapNav, NavID), SAM segmentation, CLIP features, Redis (Shared Memory), Edge-Cloud publish-subscribe architecture. | The MVP involves real-world demonstrations in three scenarios: (1) A restaurant where a Unitree G1 humanoid and AgileX dual-arm robot collaborate to make and deliver a burger; (2) A household where Realman single-arm and AgileX dual-arm robots fetch an orange and knife; (3) A supermarket where robots coordinate to select and package a gift into a bag. | 1\. Achieves true cross-embodiment compatibility, allowing completely different robots to collaborate seamlessly.2\. Overcomes the long-horizon planning limitations of end-to-end VLA models through hierarchical task decomposition.3\. Features dynamic error correction and real-time replanning via continuous feedback loops.4\. Ultra-low latency edge-cloud communication (command response \< 0.001s). | 1\. Current real-world experiments are limited to restaurants, households, and supermarkets; untested in complex industrial settings (e.g., assembly lines, logistics hubs).2\. High computational overhead required for cloud-based RoboBrain inference.3\. Dependency on accurate real-time 3D scene graph construction, which can be challenging in highly unstructured or occluded environments. |
| 3 | Applying Theory of Mind to Multi-Agent Systems: A Systematic Review | N/A (Systematic Review Paper, likely conference proceeding/journal based on formatting and references) | N/A (Not provided in text) | This paper presents a systematic literature review following PRISMA guidelines to analyze how the cognitive science concept of "Theory of Mind" (ToM)—the ability to attribute mental states like beliefs, desires, and intentions to others—has been applied to Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) between 2002 and 2022\. Out of 698 initially identified papers, 60 were selected for deep analysis. The review answers 9 research questions, revealing that most MAS research focuses on modeling standard BDI (Beliefs-Desires-Intentions) architectures using formal logic representations. While applications span games, negotiations, and human-agent interaction, most works remain theoretical proofs-of-concept. The paper identifies significant challenges, including handling the dynamism of real-world environments, scaling ToM inferences, and capturing implicit beliefs. It concludes by mapping cognitive ToM theories (Theory-Theory, Simulation Theory) to AI paradigms (Symbolic, Subsymbolic, Neurosymbolic), highlighting a gap in achieving realistic, human-like mentalization in agent-agent interactions. | Formal representations including Epistemic Logic, First-Order Logic (FOL), Propositional Logic, Bayesian Modeling, Markov Decision Processes (MDMs), and POMDPs. | Agent-Oriented Programming Languages (JaCaMo, 2APL, JADE), Machine Learning (Neural Networks, Deep Learning, LSTMs), Probabilistic frameworks, 2D/3D Simulation Platforms, Robotics middleware (ROS), Ontologies (Ontologenius). | N/A \- This is a review paper. However, the most common proof-of-concept MVPs identified in the reviewed literature include: (1) Virtual agents in games (e.g., Hanabi) using ToM to outperform non-ToM agents; (2) Personal assistant chatbots modeling human user beliefs to improve interaction; (3) Decision-theoretic agents in simulated social dilemmas (e.g., Disaster World, PsychSim). | 1\. Provides a comprehensive, structured mapping of 20 years of research at the intersection of cognitive science and MAS.2\. Clearly identifies the dominant paradigms (BDI, formal logic) and maps them to cognitive theories.3\. Highlights critical research gaps, such as the lack of neurosymbolic (Hybrid Theory) implementations in MAS.4\. Establishes a clear agenda for developing more human-like social intelligence in AI. | 1\. More than half of the reviewed papers failed to explicitly report their limitations or challenges.2\. The majority of the 60 reviewed works are theoretical or rely on highly controlled simulations, lacking real-world validation.3\. Many empirical studies suffered from small sample sizes (both in terms of human participants and agent populations).4\. Current mathematical and logical frameworks are noted as inadequate for fully capturing the nuances of human ToM. |
| Sr. No. | Name of the paper | Publication | Doi | Detailed Summary | Mathematics used | Technology stack used | MVP of it | Advantages | Disadvantages |
| 1 | Can Virtual AI Agents Improve Cognitive Assessment? A Virtual Reality Perspective | The PErvasive Technologies Related to Assistive Environments (PETRA ’25), June 25–27, 2025, Corfu Island, Greece | 10.1145/3733155.3734917 | This paper introduces "SmartSage," an intelligent virtual AI agent designed to enhance cognitive assessment, specifically for ADHD, by integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision Language Models (VLMs) within a Virtual Reality (VR) environment. Built on the clinically validated Automated Test of Embodied Cognition (ATEC) framework, the system uses the Meta Quest platform to track motion and provide haptic feedback. SmartSage guides users through two physical tasks—Pass the Ball to the Beat (PBB) and Cross Your Body (CYB)—delivering real-time demonstrations, personalized feedback, and dynamically adjusting task difficulty based on user performance. A pilot study with 8 participants demonstrated that while traditional video guides (Eliza) were initially easier to understand, SmartSage provided superior feedback quality, higher engagement, and greater user confidence, indicating strong potential for accessible and ecologically valid cognitive assessments. | Rhythmic accuracy metrics, movement precision calculations, response timing analysis, and statistical evaluation of user survey data (Likert scale agreement distributions). | Meta Quest VR Headset and Controllers (Motion tracking & Haptics), Unity Game Engine, Large Language Models (Phi 3), Vision Language Models (PaliGemma 2), Machine Learning algorithms for real-time data analysis. | The working prototype MVP is the SmartSage VR application itself. It features a virtual AI avatar guiding users through two distinct ATEC tasks (PBB and CYB) in a VR room, capable of interpreting visual cues, tracking physical movements via controllers, and dynamically scaling task difficulty in real-time. | 1\. Maintains clinical validity while significantly boosting user engagement through immersive VR.2\. Provides personalized, real-time adaptive feedback, unlike static video instructions.3\. Evaluates both task accuracy and rhythm accuracy, offering a more nuanced understanding of ADHD than screen-based tests.4\. High ecological validity due to the integration of embodied cognition (physical movement). | 1\. Initial task explanation phase is less effective than straightforward traditional video guides.2\. Variability in user comfort levels when interacting with VR controllers.3\. Pilot study sample size was very small (8 participants).4\. Lacks extensive clinical validation across diverse populations and neurodivergent profiles at this stage. |

Validated in real-world scenarios (restaurants, households, supermarkets) using diverse robots (humanoids, single/dual-arm, wheeled), RoboOS successfully demonstrated complex collaborative tasks like cross-embodiment burger preparation and gift packaging. | Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) fusion formulas, Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAG) for topological subtask allocation, 3D scene graph algorithms for spatial memory, Discrete Fréchet Distance (DFD), Hausdorff Distance (HD), and RMSE for trajectory evaluation. | Cloud-based distributed inference (FlagScale framework), Qwen2.5-VL-7B (MLLM base), OpenVLA, RDT-1B, π0 (VLA tools), SLAM, Vision-Language-Navigation (MapNav, NavID), SAM segmentation, CLIP features, Redis (Shared Memory), Edge-Cloud publish-subscribe architecture. | The MVP involves real-world demonstrations in three scenarios: (1) A restaurant where a Unitree G1 humanoid and AgileX dual-arm robot collaborate to make and deliver a burger; (2) A household where Realman single-arm and AgileX dual-arm robots fetch an orange and knife; (3) A supermarket where robots coordinate to select and package a gift into a bag. | 1\. Achieves true cross-embodiment compatibility, allowing completely different robots to collaborate seamlessly.2\. Overcomes the long-horizon planning limitations of end-to-end VLA models through hierarchical task decomposition.3\. Features dynamic error correction and real-time replanning via continuous feedback loops.4\. Ultra-low latency edge-cloud communication (command response \< 0.001s). | 1\. Current real-world experiments are limited to restaurants, households, and supermarkets; untested in complex industrial settings (e.g., assembly lines, logistics hubs).2\. High computational overhead required for cloud-based RoboBrain inference.3\. Dependency on accurate real-time 3D scene graph construction, which can be challenging in highly unstructured or occluded environments. |
| 3 | Applying Theory of Mind to Multi-Agent Systems: A Systematic Review | N/A (Systematic Review Paper, likely conference proceeding/journal based on formatting and references) | N/A (Not provided in text) | This paper presents a systematic literature review following PRISMA guidelines to analyze how the cognitive science concept of "Theory of Mind" (ToM)—the ability to attribute mental states like beliefs, desires, and intentions to others—has been applied to Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) between 2002 and 2022\. Out of 698 initially identified papers, 60 were selected for deep analysis. The review answers 9 research questions, revealing that most MAS research focuses on modeling standard BDI (Beliefs-Desires-Intentions) architectures using formal logic representations. While applications span games, negotiations, and human-agent interaction, most works remain theoretical proofs-of-concept. The paper identifies significant challenges, including handling the dynamism of real-world environments, scaling ToM inferences, and capturing implicit beliefs. It concludes by mapping cognitive ToM theories (Theory-Theory, Simulation Theory) to AI paradigms (Symbolic, Subsymbolic, Neurosymbolic), highlighting a gap in achieving realistic, human-like mentalization in agent-agent interactions. | Formal representations including Epistemic Logic, First-Order Logic (FOL), Propositional Logic, Bayesian Modeling, Markov Decision Processes (MDMs), and POMDPs. | Agent-Oriented Programming Languages (JaCaMo, 2APL, JADE), Machine Learning (Neural Networks, Deep Learning, LSTMs), Probabilistic frameworks, 2D/3D Simulation Platforms, Robotics middleware (ROS), Ontologies (Ontologenius). | N/A \- This is a review paper. However, the most common proof-of-concept MVPs identified in the reviewed literature include: (1) Virtual agents in games (e.g., Hanabi) using ToM to outperform non-ToM agents; (2) Personal assistant chatbots modeling human user beliefs to improve interaction; (3) Decision-theoretic agents in simulated social dilemmas (e.g., Disaster World, PsychSim). | 1\. Provides a comprehensive, structured mapping of 20 years of research at the intersection of cognitive science and MAS.2\. Clearly identifies the dominant paradigms (BDI, formal logic) and maps them to cognitive theories.3\. Highlights critical research gaps, such as the lack of neurosymbolic (Hybrid Theory) implementations in MAS.4\. Establishes a clear agenda for developing more human-like social intelligence in AI. | 1\. More than half of the reviewed papers failed to explicitly report their limitations or challenges.2\. The majority of the 60 reviewed works are theoretical or rely on highly controlled simulations, lacking real-world validation.3\. Many empirical studies suffered from small sample sizes (both in terms of human participants and agent populations).4\. Current mathematical and logical frameworks are noted as inadequate for fully capturing the nuances of human ToM. |
| Sr. No. | Name of the paper | Publication | Doi | Detailed Summary | Mathematics used | Technology stack used | MVP of it | Advantages | Disadvantages |
| 1 | Can Virtual AI Agents Improve Cognitive Assessment? A Virtual Reality Perspective | The PErvasive Technologies Related to Assistive Environments (PETRA ’25), June 25–27, 2025, Corfu Island, Greece | 10.1145/3733155.3734917 | This paper introduces "SmartSage," an intelligent virtual AI agent designed to enhance cognitive assessment, specifically for ADHD, by integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision Language Models (VLMs) within a Virtual Reality (VR) environment. Built on the clinically validated Automated Test of Embodied Cognition (ATEC) framework, the system uses the Meta Quest platform to track motion and provide haptic feedback. SmartSage guides users through two physical tasks—Pass the Ball to the Beat (PBB) and Cross Your Body (CYB)—delivering real-time demonstrations, personalized feedback, and dynamically adjusting task difficulty based on user performance. A pilot study with 8 participants demonstrated that while traditional video guides (Eliza) were initially easier to understand, SmartSage provided superior feedback quality, higher engagement, and greater user confidence, indicating strong potential for accessible and ecologically valid cognitive assessments. | Rhythmic accuracy metrics, movement precision calculations, response timing analysis, and statistical evaluation of user survey data (Likert scale agreement distributions). | Meta Quest VR Headset and Controllers (Motion tracking & Haptics), Unity Game Engine, Large Language Models (Phi 3), Vision Language Models (PaliGemma 2), Machine Learning algorithms for real-time data analysis. | The working prototype MVP is the SmartSage VR application itself. It features a virtual AI avatar guiding users through two distinct ATEC tasks (PBB and CYB) in a VR room, capable of interpreting visual cues, tracking physical movements via controllers, and dynamically scaling task difficulty in real-time. | 1\. Maintains clinical validity while significantly boosting user engagement through immersive VR.2\. Provides personalized, real-time adaptive feedback, unlike static video instructions.3\. Evaluates both task accuracy and rhythm accuracy, offering a more nuanced understanding of ADHD than screen-based tests.4\. High ecological validity due to the integration of embodied cognition (physical movement). | 1\. Initial task explanation phase is less effective than straightforward traditional video guides.2\. Variability in user comfort levels when interacting with VR controllers.3\. Pilot study sample size was very small (8 participants).4\. Lacks extensive clinical validation across diverse populations and neurodivergent profiles at this stage. |
