|  1 |  Designing Agent-Oriented Systems |  The Agents Journey, LNCS 16395, pp. 213–240, 2026 |  10.1007/978-3-032-22940-3\_8 |  This chapter provides a comprehensive 25-year retrospective of Agent-Oriented Software Engineering (AOSE), analyzing trends primarily from the Workshop on Objects and Agents (WOA). It traces the evolution of agent design from simple state-chart-based reactive systems (e.g., GAIA, PASSI, JADE) to complex Belief-Desire-Intention (BDI) cognitive models (e.g., TROPOS, PRACTIONIST) and finally to modern systems capable of run-time introspection and self-adaptation. The paper breaks down AOSE into core concepts: Autonomy/Proactivity (moving from tasks to goal-oriented run-time models), Situatedness (using ontologies and nature-inspired models), and Sociality (structural, functional, and deontic dimensions via Moise+). It also explores the shift towards Method Engineering, where methodologies are constructed by assembling reusable "method fragments" from a repository, guided by metamodels (like MOF/UML) and notations (like AUML) to bridge the gap between conceptual design and implementation. |  No complex computational mathematics; relies heavily on Discrete Mathematics, Graph Theory, and Set Theory for modeling metamodels, structural relationships (holons), and method fragment dependencies (e.g., relational data structures for fragment repositories: Fragment (ID, FragName, FileName, ID Phase)). |  UML, MOF (Meta-Object Facility), SPEM (Software Process Engineering Metamodel), AUML (Agent UML), JADE, FIPA-OS, AgentSpeak, Jason, MetaMeth (CAME/CAPE tool), JaWE workflow editor. |  A structured, repository-based methodology framework that allows software engineers to dynamically assemble customized, ad-hoc agent-oriented design processes using reusable method fragments, metamodels, and standardized notations. |  1\. Modularity & Reusability: Method fragments allow developers to avoid reinventing methodologies for every new project.2\. Bridging Design & Code: Strong emphasis on metamodels and MDE ensures seamless translation from high-level agent abstractions to executable code.3\. Historical Continuity: Successfully maps the transition from object-oriented paradigms to complex intentional agent systems.4\. Standardization: Pushes for FIPA-compliant documentation and unified modeling notations (AUML). |  1\. Declining Adoption Trend: Analysis shows a sharp drop in AOSE publications post-2018, suggesting a struggle to keep pace with modern data-driven AI.2\. Complexity Overhead: The sheer number of metamodels, fragments, and notations can be overwhelming and overly bureaucratic for practical, agile development.3\. Limited Deontic Modeling: Very few methodologies explicitly handle the "deontic" (rules/constraints/punishments) dimension of agent societies. |
|  2  |  25 Years of Declarative Agent Technologies in Italy |  The Agents Journey, LNCS 16395, pp. 287–319, 2026 |  10.1007/978-3-032-22940-3\_11 |  This chapter reviews 25 years of research on declarative and logic-based multi-agent systems (MAS), drawing heavily from WOA publications. Unlike black-box AI, declarative approaches focus on "what" needs to be done rather than "how," offering transparency, explainability, and formal verification. The paper categorizes works into logic-based agents (e.g., DALI, DCaseLP), cognitive/BDI agents (Jason, JaCaMo), formal verification (model checking, runtime verification via SWI-Prolog), semantic web/ontologies (JADEL, OWL integrations), and argumentation. A significant portion traces the evolution of the authors' own "CaseLP" environment into "VEsNA"—a modern framework integrating BDI agents (JaCaMo), virtual environments (Unity/Godot), chatbots (Dialogflow/Rasa), and runtime verification (RML/SWI-Prolog) to manage Virtual Environments via Natural language Agents, bridging symbolic AI with modern HCI. |  Formal Logic, Linear Logic, Temporal Logic, and Graph Theory. Specifically: Propositional/First-Order Logic for agent specification; Linear Logic (e.g., Ehhf language) for state/resources; Alternating-time Temporal Logic (ATL) with resource bounds for model checking; Logic Programming (Prolog) for protocol conformance and argumentation graphs. |  Prolog, SWI-Prolog, Jason, CArtAgO, MOISE, JaCaMo, JENA (for OWL), Unity, Godot, Dialogflow, Rasa, RML (Runtime Monitoring Language), NetLogo, ROS 2\. |  A transparent, logically grounded, and formally verifiable alternative to black-box LLMs, culminating in frameworks like VEsNA that merge classic symbolic BDI reasoning with modern virtual reality and natural language interfaces for Trustworthy AI. |  1\. Explainability & Trust: Unlike neural networks, declarative rules allow explicit inspection of why an agent made a decision, aligning with EU Trustworthy AI guidelines.2\. Formal Verification: Guarantees system safety and correctness via exhaustive model checking and runtime monitoring.3\. Fairness & Bias Correction: Explicit rule-based logic makes it easier to identify and manually correct algorithmic biases.4\. Modularity: Easy to update rules without retraining entire models. |  1\. Scalability Issues: Logic programming and formal verification often face computational bottlenecks when applied to massive, real-world datasets.2\. Knowledge Acquisition Bottleneck: Manually writing logical rules and ontologies is extremely time-consuming compared to data-driven learning.3\. Limited Sub-symbolic Integration: While neuro-symbolic integration is highlighted as a future direction, current implementations still struggle to seamlessly blend learning-based perception with logic-based reasoning. |
|  3 |  Large Language Model based Multi-Agents: A Survey of Progress and Challenges |  arXiv:2402.01680v2 \[cs.CL\] (Preprint / Survey) |  arXiv:2402.01680v2 |  This comprehensive survey dissects the rapidly evolving field of LLM-based Multi-Agent (LLM-MA) systems. It moves beyond single-agent LLMs by profiling multiple agents with distinct roles to solve complex tasks collaboratively or simulate worlds. The paper introduces a structured schema analyzing LLM-MA through four lenses: 1\) Agent-Environment Interface (Sandbox, Physical, None), 2\) Agent Profiling (Pre-defined, Model-Generated, Data-Derived), 3\) Agent Communication (Paradigms: Cooperative/Debate/Competitive; Structures: Layered/Decentralized/Centralized/Shared Message Pool), and 4\) Capability Acquisition (Memory, Self-Evolution, Dynamic Generation). Applications are bifurcated into Problem Solving (software dev, embodied robots, science debates) and World Simulation (societal behavior, gaming, economics, psychology, policy making, disease propagation). The survey highlights tools like MetaGPT, CAMEL, and AutoGen, and lists essential benchmarks (e.g., HumanEval, SOTOPIA) while identifying future challenges like Agent Orchestration. |  No specific mathematical proofs, but relies conceptually on Graph Theory (for communication structures and social networks), Probability & Statistics (for sampling agent profiles, economic simulations, and psychological behavioral distributions), and Reinforcement Learning formulations (for integrating RL policies with LLM agents in gaming). |  GPT-4, ChatGPT, Llama, ChatArena, MetaGPT, CAMEL, AutoGen, Code Interpreters, ROS (for robotics), MovieLens-1M (for recommender simulations), OpenAI Gym. |  A universal, LLM-driven collaborative framework where specialized, communicative AI agents can seamlessly emulate human-like teamwork to solve complex real-world problems or generate massive, realistic social/economic simulations. |  1\. Emergent Collective Intelligence: Multi-agent debate and cooperation consistently outperform single LLMs in reasoning and factuality tasks.2\. Versatile Simulation Capabilities: Unprecedented ability to simulate thousands of humans for social science, economic, and psychological studies at scale.3\. Specialization without Fine-tuning: Prompt-driven profiling allows a single base LLM to act as diverse experts (coder, PM, doctor) simultaneously. Scalability: Dynamic generation allows systems to spawn new agents on-the-fly to handle sudden task complexity. |  1\. Hallucination Propagation: Errors made by one agent can quickly propagate and amplify through agent communication networks.2\. High Computational Cost: Running multiple instances of large LLMs simultaneously is economically and computationally expensive.3\. Orchestration Complexity: Managing communication structures, preventing infinite debate loops, and coordinating large swarms of agents remains a massive engineering challenge.4\. Lack of Formal Guarantees: Unlike declarative systems, LLM-MA systems are black boxes; there is no formal verification of safety or alignment, making them risky for high-stakes domains. |
| Sr. No. | Name of the paper | Publication | Doi | Detailed Summary | Mathematics used | Technology stack used | MVP of it (Core Value Proposition) | Advantages | Disadvantages |
| 1 | On Multi-Agent Cognitive Cooperation: Can virtual agents behave like humans? | Neurocomputing (Volume 480, 2022\) | 10.1016/j.neucom.2022.01.025 | This paper presents a two-fold study investigating cognitive collaboration among humans and virtual agents. In the human experiment, pairs use a custom 3-DOF haptic interface (GRAB) to collaboratively navigate a 3D maze. Visual information is split: one user sees walls/goals, the other sees bonuses/maluses. Their haptic inputs combine to control a single avatar. The study proves that multi-modal (visual+haptic) feedback allows humans to build a "Common Frame of Reference" (CFR) for cognitive collaboration. In the machine learning experiment, this human dynamic is mimicked using a novel Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) setting. Two independent tabular Q-learning agents control one 2D avatar, each perceiving different complementary cues (walls vs. bombs). Three reward policies were tested. The "Two-step reward" policy (rewarding agents when their actions match) successfully forced the agents to learn each other's intentions and establish a CFR, proving virtual agents can mimic human cognitive cooperation. | Markov Decision Processes (MDPs) and Markov Games:1\. MDP Tuple:  (S,A,P,R) for state space, action space, transition probability, and reward function.2\. Discounted Accumulated Reward:  ∑t≥0​γtRat​​(st​,st+1​) where  γ∈\[0,1\] is the discount factor.3\. Q-Learning Update Rule (Bellman Equation):  Qnew​=Qcurr​(st​,at​)+α\[rt​+γmaxa​Q(st+1​,a)−Qcurr​(st​,at​)\] 4\. Score Function (Human Exp):  score=10×nstars​−15×nbombs​+20×(maxtime−t) | GRAB Haptic Interface (3 actuated DOF, 2R+1P), Virtual Reality Environment, Distributed Virtual Machines (VMs), UDP Protocol (1kHz haptic loop), Tabular Q-Learning Algorithm, 2D Discrete Grid Space (8x8). | A novel MARL paradigm where independent agents with heterogeneous, complementary perceptions learn to implicitly cooperate and infer each other's intentions to control a single shared avatar, closely mimicking human cognitive collaboration. | 1\. Novel MARL Formulation: Introduces a unique shared-avatar control mechanism rather than traditional independent multi-agent navigation.2\. Human-Centric Validation: Directly compares and maps human cognitive behavior (CFR) to machine learning reward mechanisms.3\. Robust Haptic Integration: Successfully uses haptics not just for feedback, but as the primary cognitive driver for collaboration. | 1\. Scalability Limitations: Tabular Q-learning restricts the approach to small, discrete state spaces (8x8 grid); unfeasible for complex, continuous real-world environments.2\. Diagonal Movement Issue: In standard reward settings, joint independent actions lead to unfeasible diagonal movements in discrete grids.3\. Haptic Learning Curve: Human participants struggled initially with haptic devices, requiring multiple trials to establish cognitive flow. |
| 2 | Cognitive digital twin-enabled multi-robot collaborative manufacturing: Framework and approaches | Computers & Industrial Engineering (Volume 194, 2024\) | 10.1016/j.cie.2024.110418 | This paper proposes a Cognitive Digital Twin (CDT) framework to bridge the gap between the digitization and intelligence of Multi-Robot Collaborative Manufacturing (MRCMfg). The framework consists of three spaces: 1\) Physical-Virtual Space (MAS-based DT creating robot, task, and workstation agents), 2\) Cognition Space (Multi-Behavior Tree models unifying knowledge representation for all agents), and 3\) Data Space (Data-driven cognition evolution using DL, Transfer Learning, and RL). To handle complex manufacturing environments (e.g., screw tightening/loosening), the paper introduces IMTDAE (Incomplete Multimodal Transformer with Deep Autoencoder). IMTDAE solves the issue of missing sensor data by using a correlation matrix and DAEs to reconstruct missing modalities before feeding the 8-modality data into a Transformer for robust state perception. Knowledge transfer adapts assembly models to disassembly tasks, and DQN handles dynamic subtask sequencing. | Transformer Architecture & Deep Learning Mathematics:1\. Data Modality Repair via Softmax Weighting:  jrepairth​=∑k=ac​eCork,j​eCora,j​​×jath​+... 2\. Markov Decision Process (MDP) for RL: Tuple  (S,A,P,γ,R,γ) where  S is the observation matrix (robot position & subtask states) and  A is one-hot encoded subtask selection.3\. Behavior Trees (BT): Mathematical representation using directed acyclic graphs with composite nodes (Sequence, Selector, Parallel) returning Success/Failure/Running states.4\. Deep Autoencoder (DAE): Loss function optimized to map input  x to output  x\~ for data correlation indexing. | UR5e & UR5 Robots, Onrobot Screwdriver, Robotiq 2F-140 Gripper, Mujoco (Simulation), ROS, URScript, IMTDAE (Transformer with 12 encoder blocks, 768-dim embeddings), Deep Q-Network (DQN). | A unified CDT framework that seamlessly integrates heterogeneous digital twin models with knowledge-driven Behavior Trees and data-driven AI (Transformers, RL) to give multi-robot manufacturing systems autonomous perception, transfer learning, and decision-making capabilities. | 1\. Unified Cognition Model: Behavior Trees successfully bridge the gap between knowledge-driven engineering and data-driven AI, making the system interpretable.2\. High Robustness: IMTDAE effectively handles missing or asynchronous multimodal sensor data, a common issue in real-world manufacturing.3\. Efficient Knowledge Transfer: Solves the data-deficiency problem in disassembly tasks by fine-tuning assembly models using Transfer Learning. | 1\. Simulation-to-Reality (Sim2Real) Gap: While validated in a physical setup, the RL and Transformer models rely heavily on virtual simulation (Mujoco) which may not capture all real-world physics/friction.2\. Computational Overhead: Processing 8-modality data through a 12-block Transformer encoder and multiple DAEs requires significant computing power, potentially causing latency in real-time manufacturing.3\. Task Specificity: The cognitive evolution approaches (like DQN subtask planning) are highly tailored to the screw-loosening scenario and may need complete retraining for different assembly tasks. |
| 3 | Past, Present and Future Trends in Multi-Agent System Technology | Journal Européen des Systèmes Automatisés (Vol. 55, No. 6, 2022\) | 10.18280/jesa.550604 | This paper provides a comprehensive review and tutorial on Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) technology, tracing its roots from Distributed Artificial Intelligence (DAI) in the 1970s to modern applications. It systematically breaks down MAS into its foundational components: defining what an "agent" is (entity, environment, parameters, actions) and detailing agent taxonomies based on perception, reasoning, memory, and dynamic/interaction criteria (e.g., simple reflex, model-based, goal-based, utility-based, and learning agents). The paper categorizes modern MAS features (leadership, mobility, topology, heterogeneity, agreement parameters, time delays, and event/time-triggered data transmission). Furthermore, it reviews and compares 12 modern MAS development frameworks (ASTRA, GOAL, Jason, JADE, JADEL, Jadex, LightJason, JaCaMo, etc.) based on their underlying models (BDI, Rule-based, FIPA) and implementation languages. Finally, it outlines MAS applications in supply chain, healthcare, and manufacturing, predicting future trends. | Graph Theory and Discrete Mathematics:1\. State Spaces & Topologies: Mathematical representation of static vs. dynamic topologies using graph theory nodes and edges.2\. Markov Chains: Referenced in the Chromar framework (Continuous-Time Markov Chain \- CTMC) for stochastic semantics.3\. Matrices for Agreement Parameters: Utilizing first-order, second-order, and high-order matrices for agent consensus and agreement metrics.4\. Logic Programming: Mathematical logic foundations for BDI (Belief-Desire-Intention) architectures and Prolog-based knowledge bases. | ASTRA, Chromar (Haskell), GOAL (Java/Prolog), Jason (Java), Gwendolen (Java), JADE (Java), JADEL (Xtext/Xtend), Jadex (Java/XML), LightJason (Java), JaCaMo, MaSMT (Java), PLACE (Java APIs). | A holistic, educational reference blueprint that categorizes the entire MAS ecosystem—from agent psychology/classification to the comparative analysis of modern programming frameworks—serving as a foundational guide for researchers and developers entering the MAS domain. | 1\. Highly Comprehensive Taxonomy: Offers one of the most detailed breakdowns of agent types based on multiple attributive dimensions (dynamic, interactive, autonomous, learning, cooperative).2\. Framework Comparison: Provides a clear, tabulated comparison of 12 active MAS frameworks, helping practitioners choose the right tool (e.g., JADE for FIPA compliance, JaCaMo for organizational modeling).3\. Historical Context: Excellently maps the transition from classical AI to DAI to modern MAS. | 1\. Lack of Empirical Evaluation: As a review/tutorial paper, it does not provide experimental validations, performance benchmarks, or mathematical proofs of the discussed frameworks.2\. Omission of Modern LLM-based MAS: Published in 2022, it entirely misses the recent paradigm shift of integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) as cognitive agents (e.g., AutoGen, CAMEL).3\. Broad but Shallow: Due to the vast scope, deep mathematical algorithms underlying specific frameworks (like RL in Jadex or HTN in PLACE) are not explored in detail. |
| 1 | Immersive and interactive cyber-physical system (I2CPS) and virtual reality interface for human involved robotic manufacturing | Journal of Manufacturing Systems (Volume 62, 2022\) | 10.1016/j.jmsy.2021.11.018 | This paper proposes the Immersive and Interactive Cyber-Physical System (I2CPS) framework to bridge the gap between human operators, autonomous systems (AI/digital twins), and physical manufacturing assets. Traditional HMIs (2D screens, pendants) are not intuitive and separate autonomy from the operator. I2CPS combines M2M middleware (ROS) for fast, raw data transfer and a data-to-information protocol (MTConnect) for structured, human-readable contextual data. This dual-pipeline feeds an immersive Virtual Reality (VR) interface built in a game engine (Unity). The framework enables three main collaborative applications: 1\) Intuitive robot manipulation via VR controllers using inverse kinematics, 2\) Augmentation of human work accuracy through virtual constraints (e.g., haptic feedback boundaries, variable space mapping for precision vs. speed), and 3\) Virtual robot teaching, where a human's VR trajectory is recorded and automatically converted into executable industrial robot code (digital model), effectively allowing humans to teach autonomy. | Robot Kinematics & Linear Algebra:1\. Homogeneous Transformation Matrices:  T06​=T01​T12​…T56​=\[R0​t1​\] , where  R is the rotation matrix,  t is translation, and  rij​=f(q,η) .2\. Inverse Kinematics Geometric Method:  (T01​T12​T23​)−1T06​=T43​T54​T65​ used to solve 6 joint angles from a desired end-effector pose.3\. Closed-loop Inverse Kinematics (Jacobian):  q˙​sd​=γ⋅JT(qsd​)⋅(xsr​−xsd​) for slave system joint velocity calculation.4\. Variable Space Mapping:  xsr​(t)=xs​(tp​)+f(x,y,z)(xm​(t)−xm​(tp​)) where  f(x,y,z) is a non-linear scaling function for precision control. | Middleware & Protocols: Robot Operating System (ROS), MTConnect (Agent & Adapters), Rosbridge (WebSocket/JSON/TCP), XML.Hardware: Samsung Odyssey+ VR Headset, 3D Systems Touch Haptic Pen, Stäubli TX2-40, KUKA KR6 R700, Doosan M0609, Sawyer Robots.Software: Unity Game Engine (C\# scripting), URDF (Unified Robot Description Format), STL/Mesh modeling. | A unified VR-based collaborative framework that seamlessly fuses raw sensor data (via ROS) with contextualized manufacturing data (via MTConnect), enabling humans to intuitively visualize, constrain, and digitize their physical work into autonomous robot programs. | 1\. Enhanced Human Cognition: VR provides intuitive 3D depth perception and visual aids (e.g., color-coding joint torques) that 2D screens cannot offer.2\. Error Prevention: Virtual constraints (haptic boundaries, rendered working volumes) prevent operators from sending out-of-bounds commands that could trigger physical robot alarms or collisions.3\. Seamless Data Contextualization: Splitting ROS (fast raw data) and MTConnect (structured XML history) optimizes the system for both real-time rendering and long-term AI/predictive analysis.4\. Democratizing Programming: Allows non-expert workers to program industrial robots simply by performing tasks in VR (virtual teaching). | 1\. Communication Latency: The VR-to-robot control refresh rate is bottlenecked to \~0.5s (5 Hz) in controller mode due to inverse kinematics calculations and internal robot controller logic, limiting real-time teleoperation.2\. MTConnect Limitations: MTConnect is a read-only protocol, meaning a separate duplex channel (ROS) must be maintained purely for sending commands back to the physical machines.3\. Hardware Dependency: The framework's intuitiveness heavily relies on high-fidelity VR headsets and precise haptic devices, which can be expensive and cause physical fatigue for operators over long shifts. |
